I guess… the main thing is I keep hearing this man’s voice. Yeah… I think so. Not always tied to being really up or really down, if that’s what you mean.

Not every second, but it was still happening, coming through like the vents, and I still felt like my husband and my boss were tracking me somehow. Which—sorry, I know that sounds like I’m jumping around, but yes, it wasn’t just when my mood was all over the place. It kept going during a more normal couple weeks too.\n\nDoctor: When the voice started, had you been using methamphetamine or another drug?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I mean, I know what you’re asking, but I don’t have, like, a clean answer in my head.\n\nI wasn’t keeping a calendar of it or anything. Around that time everything was kind of messy, work was messy, home was messy, and I was just trying to get through shifts. I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure, and I don’t want to say no and have it be wrong either.\n\nWhen you say “using,” do you mean like right that day, or in the weeks around it? Because I’m not totally sure how to separate all that out.\n\nDoctor: Around the time the voice first started, were you using methamphetamine?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know for sure. That whole time is kind of… smudged together, if that makes sense. Like I remember the shop being chaotic and me trying to keep track of parts orders, and then my husband asking me stuff at home, and I’d be thinking about whether I left a socket set in the wrong bay—\n\nSorry. I’m not trying to dodge it. I just can’t honestly say, “yes, I was using right when it started,” because I don’t know. And I can’t swear “no” either. I wasn’t keeping track like that.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice and the feeling that your husband and boss were tracking you been going on for six months or longer?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been longer than six months. I mean, I keep trying to count it from when I first noticed it in the garage, because time gets weird when you’re just going to work and coming home and doing the same stupid laundry pile—sorry.\n\nBut yeah, it’s been about ten months now, the voice and the feeling like they were tracking me. It hasn’t really just gone away.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the times when your mood was high or low, have you had a long period where you felt emotionally flat or had very little motivation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not always tied to being really up or really down, if that’s what you mean.\n\nThere are these stretches where I just… don’t call people back. Like my phone will ring and I’ll look at it and think, I should answer that, and then I just don’t. I used to help with the church potlucks, like setting up tables and making sure the coffee urn wasn’t leaking all over the floor—because that happened once and everybody acted like it was some big disaster—but now I just skip them.\n\nAnd at work, or even at home, I’ll just sit there staring at tools or whatever’s in front of me, like I know what I’m supposed to do, but I can’t make myself care enough to move. It feels flat. Like everything’s behind glass or something.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least one week when you felt unusually high, energized, or irritable most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s not like just being in a good mood, though. It’s like I’m flying high, like I can fix every car in town if people would just get out of my way.\n\nAnd then somebody says one thing—my husband asking why the dishes aren’t done, or one of the techs moves my socket set—and I just… blow up. Like they’re trying to hold me down or mess with me. I know that sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to measure “two weeks” exactly. I’ve definitely had times where I felt really down, like everything was heavy, but days all blur together sometimes.\n\nIt’s like, I’ll think it was a week, and then I remember the trash didn’t get taken out for two pickups, but our pickup day changed once because of a holiday, and then my husband said I was mixing up the weeks—so I don’t know. Which is crazy because the shop is loud, you know, compressors going, people yelling about parts, and I can still hear him.\n\nHe tells me not to trust my husband, and sometimes he says not to touch certain cars. Like I’ll be about to start on one and then there it is. I know that sounds… I don’t know. I fix cars for a living, I know what noises come from vents and engines, but this isn’t like a rattle or a belt squeal. It’s a voice.\n\nDoctor: Have you used meth, cocaine, cannabis, or any other drugs around the times when you hear this voice?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t really keep track of it like a chart or anything. Around the shop people talk, like everybody knows somebody who’s doing something, and then someone’s cousin is suddenly an expert on “what causes what,” you know? But for me, I haven’t noticed a clean line like, “oh, this happened after that.”\n\nThe voice just kind of shows up. Sometimes I’m at work, sometimes not, and I’ll be trying to focus on a job order or a stupid stripped bolt and then—yeah. So I don’t know how to answer that exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually depressed or unusually high or energized around the same period as the voice?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, there were times like that. Like I’d barely sleep, maybe a couple hours, and then I’d be at the shop moving around like I had this whole plan, this big system for how everything should run, like I could reorganize the bays and fix three impossible jobs before lunch.\n\nAnd around then the voice was there too, coming from the vents, and I’d get really sure that the chargers, like phone chargers or battery chargers, and the cameras were being used to watch me. Which sounds stupid when I say it, because I know cameras are just cameras, we have them for the lot and theft and all that, but at the time it felt connected. Like the voice and the not sleeping and the racing around all kind of came together.\n\nDoctor: Have you heard the voice during times when your mood felt normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s the part that makes me feel like I can’t just blame it on being up or down. There were a few quieter weeks where I was actually sleeping more, not doing that thing where I’m making giant plans for the whole shop or anything.\n\nAnd it still happened. The garage voice was still there, like from the vents, and I still had this feeling—well, more than a feeling at the time—that my husband and my boss were tracking me. Which is embarrassing to say, because my boss can barely track where he put the inspection stickers half the time, but… yeah, even when my mood felt more normal, it didn’t totally stop.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you were hearing the voice even though your mood felt normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There was that stretch, like a few quieter weeks, where I wasn’t doing the big “I’m gonna reorganize the whole shop” thing, and I was sleeping more. I remember because I was actually getting up more normally, making coffee, trying to be a regular person, and my husband was like, relieved, I guess.\n\nBut the garage voice was still there. Not every second, but it was still happening, coming through like the vents, and I still felt like my husband and my boss were tracking me somehow. Which—sorry, I know that sounds like I’m jumping around, but yes, it wasn’t just when my mood was all over the place. It kept going during a more normal couple weeks too.\n\nDoctor: When the voice started, had you been using methamphetamine or another drug?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I mean, I know what you’re asking, but I don’t have, like, a clean answer in my head.\n\nI wasn’t keeping a calendar of it or anything. Around that time everything was kind of messy, work was messy, home was messy, and I was just trying to get through shifts. I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure, and I don’t want to say no and have it be wrong either.\n\nWhen you say “using,” do you mean like right that day, or in the weeks around it? Because I’m not totally sure how to separate all that out.\n\nDoctor: Around the time the voice first started, were you using methamphetamine?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know for sure. That whole time is kind of… smudged together, if that makes sense. Like I remember the shop being chaotic and me trying to keep track of parts orders, and then my husband asking me stuff at home, and I’d be thinking about whether I left a socket set in the wrong bay—\n\nSorry. I’m not trying to dodge it. I just can’t honestly say, “yes, I was using right when it started,” because I don’t know. And I can’t swear “no” either. I wasn’t keeping track like that.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice and the feeling that your husband and boss were tracking you been going on for six months or longer?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been longer than six months. I mean, I keep trying to count it from when I first noticed it in the garage, because time gets weird when you’re just going to work and coming home and doing the same stupid laundry pile—sorry.\n\nBut yeah, it’s been about ten months now, the voice and the feeling like they were tracking me. It hasn’t really just gone away.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the times when your mood was high or low, have you had a long period where you felt emotionally flat or had very little motivation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not always tied to being really up or really down, if that’s what you mean.\n\nThere are these stretches where I just… don’t call people back. Like my phone will ring and I’ll look at it and think, I should answer that, and then I just don’t. I used to help with the church potlucks, like setting up tables and making sure the coffee urn wasn’t leaking all over the floor—because that happened once and everybody acted like it was some big disaster—but now I just skip them.\n\nAnd at work, or even at home, I’ll just sit there staring at tools or whatever’s in front of me, like I know what I’m supposed to do, but I can’t make myself care enough to move. It feels flat. Like everything’s behind glass or something.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least one week when you felt unusually high, energized, or irritable most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s not like just being in a good mood, though. It’s like I’m flying high, like I can fix every car in town if people would just get out of my way.\n\nAnd then somebody says one thing—my husband asking why the dishes aren’t done, or one of the techs moves my socket set—and I just… blow up. Like they’re trying to hold me down or mess with me. I know that sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to measure “two weeks” exactly. I’ve definitely had times where I felt really down, like everything was heavy, but days all blur together sometimes.\n\nIt’s like, I’ll think it was a week, and then I remember the trash didn’t get taken out for two pickups, but our pickup day changed once because of a holiday, and then my husband said I was mixing up the weeks—so I don’t know. I’m not trying to dodge it.\n\nMost of the day nearly every day… I’m not sure. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: During those down times, did you lose interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, “enjoy” is kind of a funny word for me because sometimes I’ll still do the thing, like turn a wrench or put on some show, but it’s more like it’s just happening in the room with me.\n\nDuring those down times… maybe? I don’t know if I lose interest exactly, or if I just get tired of everything asking something from me. Like even hobbies start feeling like chores, and then I start thinking about how the garage needs organizing, and how my dad used to keep every bolt in coffee cans—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t say clearly. I don’t track it well enough.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

I don’t track it well enough.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a time when you felt down or hopeless nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / automotive_service_technician_or_mechanic / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m still not sure about the two weeks part. I know I’ve had stretches where I felt really low, like everything was pointless or too much, but keeping the days straight is hard for me.

Sometimes I’ll think, “Yeah, that was forever,” but then I start trying to count by work shifts or grocery trips, and I get mixed up. Like if I missed doing laundry, was that one weekend or two? And then I’m thinking about the uniform shirts piling up, which isn’t really what you asked.

So… I can’t honestly say it was nearly every day for at least two weeks. I know I’ve felt down and hopeless at times, but I don’t know how long exactly.

Which sounds stupid when I say it, because I know cameras are just cameras, we have them for the lot and theft and all that, but at the time it felt connected. Like the voice and the not sleeping and the racing around all kind of came together.\n\nDoctor: Have you heard the voice during times when your mood felt normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s the part that makes me feel like I can’t just blame it on being up or down. There were a few quieter weeks where I was actually sleeping more, not doing that thing where I’m making giant plans for the whole shop or anything.\n\nAnd it still happened. The garage voice was still there, like from the vents, and I still had this feeling—well, more than a feeling at the time—that my husband and my boss were tracking me. Which is embarrassing to say, because my boss can barely track where he put the inspection stickers half the time, but… yeah, even when my mood felt more normal, it didn’t totally stop.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you were hearing the voice even though your mood felt normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There was that stretch, like a few quieter weeks, where I wasn’t doing the big “I’m gonna reorganize the whole shop” thing, and I was sleeping more. I remember because I was actually getting up more normally, making coffee, trying to be a regular person, and my husband was like, relieved, I guess.\n\nBut the garage voice was still there. Not every second, but it was still happening, coming through like the vents, and I still felt like my husband and my boss were tracking me somehow. Which—sorry, I know that sounds like I’m jumping around, but yes, it wasn’t just when my mood was all over the place. It kept going during a more normal couple weeks too.\n\nDoctor: When the voice started, had you been using methamphetamine or another drug?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I mean, I know what you’re asking, but I don’t have, like, a clean answer in my head.\n\nI wasn’t keeping a calendar of it or anything. I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure, and I don’t want to say no and have it be wrong either.\n\nWhen you say “using,” do you mean like right that day, or in the weeks around it? It hasn’t really just gone away.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the times when your mood was high or low, have you had a long period where you felt emotionally flat or had very little motivation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t really know. I mean, I’ve had times where food just sounded like too much effort, but I don’t know if that was no appetite or just… you know, not wanting to deal with making something.

And if I’m home, I might just eat something random, like crackers or whatever’s there, but I can’t say I stopped eating much because I had no appetite.

So… I’m not sure I noticed it that clearly.

Like the voice and the not sleeping and the racing around all kind of came together.\n\nDoctor: Have you heard the voice during times when your mood felt normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s the part that makes me feel like I can’t just blame it on being up or down. There were a few quieter weeks where I was actually sleeping more, not doing that thing where I’m making giant plans for the whole shop or anything.\n\nAnd it still happened. The garage voice was still there, like from the vents, and I still had this feeling—well, more than a feeling at the time—that my husband and my boss were tracking me. Which is embarrassing to say, because my boss can barely track where he put the inspection stickers half the time, but… yeah, even when my mood felt more normal, it didn’t totally stop.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you were hearing the voice even though your mood felt normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There was that stretch, like a few quieter weeks, where I wasn’t doing the big “I’m gonna reorganize the whole shop” thing, and I was sleeping more. I remember because I was actually getting up more normally, making coffee, trying to be a regular person, and my husband was like, relieved, I guess.\n\nBut the garage voice was still there. Not every second, but it was still happening, coming through like the vents, and I still felt like my husband and my boss were tracking me somehow. Which—sorry, I know that sounds like I’m jumping around, but yes, it wasn’t just when my mood was all over the place. It kept going during a more normal couple weeks too.\n\nDoctor: When the voice started, had you been using methamphetamine or another drug?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I mean, I know what you’re asking, but I don’t have, like, a clean answer in my head.\n\nI wasn’t keeping a calendar of it or anything. Around that time everything was kind of messy, work was messy, home was messy, and I was just trying to get through shifts. I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure, and I don’t want to say no and have it be wrong either.\n\nWhen you say “using,” do you mean like right that day, or in the weeks around it? Because I’m not totally sure how to separate all that out.\n\nDoctor: Around the time the voice first started, were you using methamphetamine?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know for sure. That whole time is kind of… smudged together, if that makes sense. Like I remember the shop being chaotic and me trying to keep track of parts orders, and then my husband asking me stuff at home, and I’d be thinking about whether I left a socket set in the wrong bay—\n\nSorry. I’m not trying to dodge it. I just can’t honestly say, “yes, I was using right when it started,” because I don’t know. And I can’t swear “no” either. I wasn’t keeping track like that.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice and the feeling that your husband and boss were tracking you been going on for six months or longer?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been longer than six months. I mean, I keep trying to count it from when I first noticed it in the garage, because time gets weird when you’re just going to work and coming home and doing the same stupid laundry pile—sorry.\n\nBut yeah, it’s been about ten months now, the voice and the feeling like they were tracking me. It hasn’t really just gone away.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the times when your mood was high or low, have you had a long period where you felt emotionally flat or had very little motivation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not always tied to being really up or really down, if that’s what you mean.\n\nThere are these stretches where I just… don’t call people back. Like my phone will ring and I’ll look at it and think, I should answer that, and then I just don’t. I used to help with the church potlucks, like setting up tables and making sure the coffee urn wasn’t leaking all over the floor—because that happened once and everybody acted like it was some big disaster—but now I just skip them.\n\nAnd at work, or even at home, I’ll just sit there staring at tools or whatever’s in front of me, like I know what I’m supposed to do, but I can’t make myself care enough to move. It feels flat. Like everything’s behind glass or something.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least one week when you felt unusually high, energized, or irritable most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s not like just being in a good mood, though. It’s like I’m flying high, like I can fix every car in town if people would just get out of my way.\n\nAnd then somebody says one thing—my husband asking why the dishes aren’t done, or one of the techs moves my socket set—and I just… blow up. Like they’re trying to hold me down or mess with me. I know that sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to measure “two weeks” exactly. I’ve definitely had times where I felt really down, like everything was heavy, but days all blur together sometimes.\n\nIt’s like, I’ll think it was a week, and then I remember the trash didn’t get taken out for two pickups, but our pickup day changed once because of a holiday, and then my husband said I was mixing up the weeks—so I don’t know. I’m not trying to dodge it.\n\nMost of the day nearly every day… I’m not sure. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: During those down times, did you lose interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, “enjoy” is kind of a funny word for me because sometimes I’ll still do the thing, like turn a wrench or put on some show, but it’s more like it’s just happening in the room with me.\n\nDuring those down times… maybe? I don’t know if I lose interest exactly, or if I just get tired of everything asking something from me. Like even hobbies start feeling like chores, and then I start thinking about how the garage needs organizing, and how my dad used to keep every bolt in coffee cans—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t say clearly. I don’t track it well enough.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt down or hopeless nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure about the two weeks part. I know I’ve had stretches where I felt really low, like everything was pointless or too much, but keeping the days straight is hard for me.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, “Yeah, that was forever,” but then I start trying to count by work shifts or grocery trips, and I get mixed up. Like if I missed doing laundry, was that one weekend or two? And then I’m thinking about the uniform shirts piling up, which isn’t really what you asked.\n\nSo… I can’t honestly say it was nearly every day for at least two weeks. I know I’ve felt down and hopeless at times, but I don’t know how long exactly.\n\nDoctor: During one of those low periods, did you feel sad or hopeless most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say “most of the day nearly every day.” I remember feeling low, yeah, like that heavy feeling where you’re just kind of dragging through things, but when you ask it that exact way I start trying to count hours, and then I get lost.\n\nLike, was I sad all day, or was I just sad in the morning before work and then numb while I was doing brakes or whatever? Sometimes at the shop you’re busy enough that you don’t really know what you feel until later, when it’s quiet. And then I’ll sit in the car too long before going inside, but I don’t know if that means most of the day.\n\nSo… I’m not saying no. I just don’t really know how to answer it cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you felt sad or hopeless nearly every day for two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking the same thing, and I’m not trying to dodge it. I just… I don’t know if I can honestly say two weeks in a row.\n\nI’ve had times where I felt really sad or hopeless, like things were just heavy and I couldn’t see the point, but when you say “nearly every day” and “two weeks,” my brain starts trying to line up the days like a calendar, and I don’t keep track that way. Then I start thinking about work weeks and whether I was on early shift or late shift, and the whole thing gets blurry.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: I’m not sure. I know I’ve had low stretches, but I can’t say for sure it was every day for two weeks straight.\n\nDoctor: During one of those low periods, did you ever stop eating much because you had no appetite?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I’ve had times where food just sounded like too much effort, but I don’t know if that was no appetite or just… you know, not wanting to deal with making something.\n\nSometimes at work I’ll forget to eat until later because we’re busy, and then I’m like, okay, is that part of the low mood or is that just the shop being chaotic? And if I’m home, I might just eat something random, like crackers or whatever’s there, but I can’t say I stopped eating much because I had no appetite.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I noticed it that clearly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I mean, I’ve had times where food just sounded like too much effort, but I don’t know if that was no appetite or just… you know, not wanting to deal with making something.\n\nSometimes at work I’ll forget to eat until later because we’re busy, and then I’m like, okay, is that part of the low mood or is that just the shop being chaotic? I don’t know what counts as “regularly,” I guess. Like, I’ve been around people where stuff gets passed around, and in the shop you hear all kinds of stories, guys acting like they can work three days straight and still torque everything right, which is stupid and dangerous, honestly.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main thing is I keep hearing this man’s voice. It sounds like it’s coming from the garage vents sometimes, and other times from under the hood when I’m working, clear like somebody’s standing right beside me. Which is crazy because the shop is loud, you know, compressors going, people yelling about parts, and I can still hear him.\n\nHe tells me not to trust my husband, and sometimes he says not to touch certain cars. Like I’ll be about to start on one and then there it is. I know that sounds… I don’t know. I fix cars for a living, I know what noises come from vents and engines, but this isn’t like a rattle or a belt squeal. But for me, I haven’t noticed a clean line like, “oh, this happened after that.”\n\nThe voice just kind of shows up. Sometimes I’m at work, sometimes not, and I’ll be trying to focus on a job order or a stupid stripped bolt and then—yeah. Like I’d barely sleep, maybe a couple hours, and then I’d be at the shop moving around like I had this whole plan, this big system for how everything should run, like I could reorganize the bays and fix three impossible jobs before lunch.\n\nAnd around then the voice was there too, coming from the vents, and I’d get really sure that the chargers, like phone chargers or battery chargers, and the cameras were being used to watch me. Which sounds stupid when I say it, because I know cameras are just cameras, we have them for the lot and theft and all that, but at the time it felt connected. Like the voice and the not sleeping and the racing around all kind of came together.\n\nDoctor: Have you heard the voice during times when your mood felt normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s the part that makes me feel like I can’t just blame it on being up or down. There were a few quieter weeks where I was actually sleeping more, not doing that thing where I’m making giant plans for the whole shop or anything.\n\nAnd it still happened. The garage voice was still there, like from the vents, and I still had this feeling—well, more than a feeling at the time—that my husband and my boss were tracking me. Which is embarrassing to say, because my boss can barely track where he put the inspection stickers half the time, but… yeah, even when my mood felt more normal, it didn’t totally stop.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you were hearing the voice even though your mood felt normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There was that stretch, like a few quieter weeks, where I wasn’t doing the big “I’m gonna reorganize the whole shop” thing, and I was sleeping more. I remember because I was actually getting up more normally, making coffee, trying to be a regular person, and my husband was like, relieved, I guess.\n\nBut the garage voice was still there. Not every second, but it was still happening, coming through like the vents, and I still felt like my husband and my boss were tracking me somehow. Which—sorry, I know that sounds like I’m jumping around, but yes, it wasn’t just when my mood was all over the place. It kept going during a more normal couple weeks too.\n\nDoctor: When the voice started, had you been using methamphetamine or another drug?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I mean, I know what you’re asking, but I don’t have, like, a clean answer in my head.\n\nI wasn’t keeping a calendar of it or anything. Around that time everything was kind of messy, work was messy, home was messy, and I was just trying to get through shifts. I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure, and I don’t want to say no and have it be wrong either.\n\nWhen you say “using,” do you mean like right that day, or in the weeks around it? Because I’m not totally sure how to separate all that out.\n\nDoctor: Around the time the voice first started, were you using methamphetamine?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know for sure. That whole time is kind of… smudged together, if that makes sense. Like I remember the shop being chaotic and me trying to keep track of parts orders, and then my husband asking me stuff at home, and I’d be thinking about whether I left a socket set in the wrong bay—\n\nSorry. I’m not trying to dodge it. I just can’t honestly say, “yes, I was using right when it started,” because I don’t know. And I can’t swear “no” either. I wasn’t keeping track like that.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice and the feeling that your husband and boss were tracking you been going on for six months or longer?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been longer than six months. I mean, I keep trying to count it from when I first noticed it in the garage, because time gets weird when you’re just going to work and coming home and doing the same stupid laundry pile—sorry.\n\nBut yeah, it’s been about ten months now, the voice and the feeling like they were tracking me. It hasn’t really just gone away.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the times when your mood was high or low, have you had a long period where you felt emotionally flat or had very little motivation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not always tied to being really up or really down, if that’s what you mean.\n\nThere are these stretches where I just… don’t call people back. Like my phone will ring and I’ll look at it and think, I should answer that, and then I just don’t. I used to help with the church potlucks, like setting up tables and making sure the coffee urn wasn’t leaking all over the floor—because that happened once and everybody acted like it was some big disaster—but now I just skip them.\n\nAnd at work, or even at home, I’ll just sit there staring at tools or whatever’s in front of me, like I know what I’m supposed to do, but I can’t make myself care enough to move. It feels flat. Like everything’s behind glass or something.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least one week when you felt unusually high, energized, or irritable most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s not like just being in a good mood, though. It’s like I’m flying high, like I can fix every car in town if people would just get out of my way.\n\nAnd then somebody says one thing—my husband asking why the dishes aren’t done, or one of the techs moves my socket set—and I just… blow up. Like they’re trying to hold me down or mess with me. I know that sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to measure “two weeks” exactly. I’ve definitely had times where I felt really down, like everything was heavy, but days all blur together sometimes.\n\nIt’s like, I’ll think it was a week, and then I remember the trash didn’t get taken out for two pickups, but our pickup day changed once because of a holiday, and then my husband said I was mixing up the weeks—so I don’t know. I’m not trying to dodge it.\n\nMost of the day nearly every day… I’m not sure. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: During those down times, did you lose interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, “enjoy” is kind of a funny word for me because sometimes I’ll still do the thing, like turn a wrench or put on some show, but it’s more like it’s just happening in the room with me.\n\nDuring those down times… maybe? I don’t know if I lose interest exactly, or if I just get tired of everything asking something from me. Like even hobbies start feeling like chores, and then I start thinking about how the garage needs organizing, and how my dad used to keep every bolt in coffee cans—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t say clearly. I don’t track it well enough.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt down or hopeless nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure about the two weeks part. I know I’ve had stretches where I felt really low, like everything was pointless or too much, but keeping the days straight is hard for me.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, “Yeah, that was forever,” but then I start trying to count by work shifts or grocery trips, and I get mixed up. Like if I missed doing laundry, was that one weekend or two? And then I’m thinking about the uniform shirts piling up, which isn’t really what you asked.\n\nSo… I can’t honestly say it was nearly every day for at least two weeks. I know I’ve felt down and hopeless at times, but I don’t know how long exactly.\n\nDoctor: During one of those low periods, did you feel sad or hopeless most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say “most of the day nearly every day.” I remember feeling low, yeah, like that heavy feeling where you’re just kind of dragging through things, but when you ask it that exact way I start trying to count hours, and then I get lost.\n\nLike, was I sad all day, or was I just sad in the morning before work and then numb while I was doing brakes or whatever? I don’t know.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring the past 10 months, did you ever hear the voice at a time when you had been off methamphetamine for several weeks?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / automotive_service_technician_or_mechanic / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Hallucinations\nmanifestation: \"What brought me in is I keep hearing a man’s voice coming from the garage vents and sometimes from under the hood, clear as somebody standing next to me, telling me not to trust my husband or touch certain cars.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I can line it up with being off meth for several weeks. That’s the part that gets muddy for me.

But the voice, yeah… that’s what scared me enough to come in. It’s this man’s voice, and it sounds like it’s coming out of the garage vents sometimes, or even from under the hood when I’m working on a car. Like clear, not just a thought in my head—like somebody standing right next to me. He’ll say not to trust my husband, or not to touch certain cars.

And then I start thinking, okay, was that during a stretch where I wasn’t using anything, or was it after I was around those people again, and I just… I can’t sort the calendar out right. But the voice has happened at work, around the cars and the vents.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF25.0, F25.9, F31.2, F20.9, F29, F06.2, F19.95\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main thing is I keep hearing this man’s voice. It sounds like it’s coming from the garage vents sometimes, and other times from under the hood when I’m working, clear like somebody’s standing right beside me. Which is crazy because the shop is loud, you know, compressors going, people yelling about parts, and I can still hear him.\n\nHe tells me not to trust my husband, and sometimes he says not to touch certain cars. Like I’ll be about to start on one and then there it is. I know that sounds… I don’t know. I fix cars for a living, I know what noises come from vents and engines, but this isn’t like a rattle or a belt squeal. It’s a voice.\n\nDoctor: Have you used meth, cocaine, cannabis, or any other drugs around the times when you hear this voice?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t really keep track of it like a chart or anything. Around the shop people talk, like everybody knows somebody who’s doing something, and then someone’s cousin is suddenly an expert on “what causes what,” you know? But for me, I haven’t noticed a clean line like, “oh, this happened after that.”\n\nThe voice just kind of shows up. Sometimes I’m at work, sometimes not, and I’ll be trying to focus on a job order or a stupid stripped bolt and then—yeah. So I don’t know how to answer that exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually depressed or unusually high or energized around the same period as the voice?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, there were times like that. Like I’d barely sleep, maybe a couple hours, and then I’d be at the shop moving around like I had this whole plan, this big system for how everything should run, like I could reorganize the bays and fix three impossible jobs before lunch.\n\nAnd around then the voice was there too, coming from the vents, and I’d get really sure that the chargers, like phone chargers or battery chargers, and the cameras were being used to watch me. Which sounds stupid when I say it, because I know cameras are just cameras, we have them for the lot and theft and all that, but at the time it felt connected. Like the voice and the not sleeping and the racing around all kind of came together.\n\nDoctor: Have you heard the voice during times when your mood felt normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s the part that makes me feel like I can’t just blame it on being up or down. There were a few quieter weeks where I was actually sleeping more, not doing that thing where I’m making giant plans for the whole shop or anything.\n\nAnd it still happened. The garage voice was still there, like from the vents, and I still had this feeling—well, more than a feeling at the time—that my husband and my boss were tracking me. Which is embarrassing to say, because my boss can barely track where he put the inspection stickers half the time, but… yeah, even when my mood felt more normal, it didn’t totally stop.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you were hearing the voice even though your mood felt normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There was that stretch, like a few quieter weeks, where I wasn’t doing the big “I’m gonna reorganize the whole shop” thing, and I was sleeping more. I remember because I was actually getting up more normally, making coffee, trying to be a regular person, and my husband was like, relieved, I guess.\n\nBut the garage voice was still there. Not every second, but it was still happening, coming through like the vents, and I still felt like my husband and my boss were tracking me somehow. Which—sorry, I know that sounds like I’m jumping around, but yes, it wasn’t just when my mood was all over the place. It kept going during a more normal couple weeks too.\n\nDoctor: When the voice started, had you been using methamphetamine or another drug?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I mean, I know what you’re asking, but I don’t have, like, a clean answer in my head.\n\nI wasn’t keeping a calendar of it or anything. Around that time everything was kind of messy, work was messy, home was messy, and I was just trying to get through shifts. I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure, and I don’t want to say no and have it be wrong either.\n\nWhen you say “using,” do you mean like right that day, or in the weeks around it? Because I’m not totally sure how to separate all that out.\n\nDoctor: Around the time the voice first started, were you using methamphetamine?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know for sure. That whole time is kind of… smudged together, if that makes sense. Like I remember the shop being chaotic and me trying to keep track of parts orders, and then my husband asking me stuff at home, and I’d be thinking about whether I left a socket set in the wrong bay—\n\nSorry. I’m not trying to dodge it. I just can’t honestly say, “yes, I was using right when it started,” because I don’t know. And I can’t swear “no” either. I wasn’t keeping track like that.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice and the feeling that your husband and boss were tracking you been going on for six months or longer?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been longer than six months. I mean, I keep trying to count it from when I first noticed it in the garage, because time gets weird when you’re just going to work and coming home and doing the same stupid laundry pile—sorry.\n\nBut yeah, it’s been about ten months now, the voice and the feeling like they were tracking me. It hasn’t really just gone away.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the times when your mood was high or low, have you had a long period where you felt emotionally flat or had very little motivation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not always tied to being really up or really down, if that’s what you mean.\n\nThere are these stretches where I just… don’t call people back. Like my phone will ring and I’ll look at it and think, I should answer that, and then I just don’t. I used to help with the church potlucks, like setting up tables and making sure the coffee urn wasn’t leaking all over the floor—because that happened once and everybody acted like it was some big disaster—but now I just skip them.\n\nAnd at work, or even at home, I’ll just sit there staring at tools or whatever’s in front of me, like I know what I’m supposed to do, but I can’t make myself care enough to move. It feels flat. Like everything’s behind glass or something.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least one week when you felt unusually high, energized, or irritable most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s not like just being in a good mood, though. It’s like I’m flying high, like I can fix every car in town if people would just get out of my way.\n\nAnd then somebody says one thing—my husband asking why the dishes aren’t done, or one of the techs moves my socket set—and I just… blow up. Like they’re trying to hold me down or mess with me. I know that sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to measure “two weeks” exactly. I’ve definitely had times where I felt really down, like everything was heavy, but days all blur together sometimes.\n\nIt’s like, I’ll think it was a week, and then I remember the trash didn’t get taken out for two pickups, but our pickup day changed once because of a holiday, and then my husband said I was mixing up the weeks—so I don’t know. I’m not trying to dodge it.\n\nMost of the day nearly every day… I’m not sure. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: During those down times, did you lose interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, “enjoy” is kind of a funny word for me because sometimes I’ll still do the thing, like turn a wrench or put on some show, but it’s more like it’s just happening in the room with me.\n\nDuring those down times… maybe? I don’t know if I lose interest exactly, or if I just get tired of everything asking something from me. Like even hobbies start feeling like chores, and then I start thinking about how the garage needs organizing, and how my dad used to keep every bolt in coffee cans—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t say clearly. I don’t track it well enough.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt down or hopeless nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure about the two weeks part. I know I’ve had stretches where I felt really low, like everything was pointless or too much, but keeping the days straight is hard for me.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, “Yeah, that was forever,” but then I start trying to count by work shifts or grocery trips, and I get mixed up. Like if I missed doing laundry, was that one weekend or two? And then I’m thinking about the uniform shirts piling up, which isn’t really what you asked.\n\nSo… I can’t honestly say it was nearly every day for at least two weeks. I know I’ve felt down and hopeless at times, but I don’t know how long exactly.\n\nDoctor: During one of those low periods, did you feel sad or hopeless most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say “most of the day nearly every day.” I remember feeling low, yeah, like that heavy feeling where you’re just kind of dragging through things, but when you ask it that exact way I start trying to count hours, and then I get lost.\n\nLike, was I sad all day, or was I just sad in the morning before work and then numb while I was doing brakes or whatever? Sometimes at the shop you’re busy enough that you don’t really know what you feel until later, when it’s quiet. And then I’ll sit in the car too long before going inside, but I don’t know if that means most of the day.\n\nSo… I’m not saying no. I just don’t really know how to answer it cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you felt sad or hopeless nearly every day for two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking the same thing, and I’m not trying to dodge it. I just… I don’t know if I can honestly say two weeks in a row.\n\nI’ve had times where I felt really sad or hopeless, like things were just heavy and I couldn’t see the point, but when you say “nearly every day” and “two weeks,” my brain starts trying to line up the days like a calendar, and I don’t keep track that way. Then I start thinking about work weeks and whether I was on early shift or late shift, and the whole thing gets blurry.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: I’m not sure. I know I’ve had low stretches, but I can’t say for sure it was every day for two weeks straight.\n\nDoctor: During one of those low periods, did you ever stop eating much because you had no appetite?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I’ve had times where food just sounded like too much effort, but I don’t know if that was no appetite or just… you know, not wanting to deal with making something.\n\nSometimes at work I’ll forget to eat until later because we’re busy, and then I’m like, okay, is that part of the low mood or is that just the shop being chaotic? And if I’m home, I might just eat something random, like crackers or whatever’s there, but I can’t say I stopped eating much because I had no appetite.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I noticed it that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you used methamphetamine regularly during the past 10 months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know what counts as “regularly,” I guess. Like, I’ve been around people where stuff gets passed around, and in the shop you hear all kinds of stories, guys acting like they can work three days straight and still torque everything right, which is stupid and dangerous, honestly.\n\nBut for me… I don’t have a clean answer for the past 10 months. I’m trying to think back month by month and it gets mixed up with work schedules and who I was hanging out with. So I don’t want to say something exact if I’m not sure what you mean by regular.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a full month when you did not use methamphetamine at all?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. A whole month feels like a big chunk to remember cleanly, and I don’t really have it marked out like that.\n\nLike, I can think of periods where I wasn’t around certain people as much, or I was just going to work and coming home, but then I start second-guessing whether that was actually a full month or just a couple weeks that felt longer. Time gets weird when you’re doing the same jobs over and over—oil changes, brakes, arguing with parts about the wrong filter showing up—and then suddenly it’s like, wait, was that March or was that after Easter?\n\nSo… I can’t honestly say yes or no to a full month with none at all. I don’t know.\n\nDoctor: During the past 10 months, did you ever hear the voice at a time when you had been off methamphetamine for several weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can line it up with being off meth for several weeks. That’s the part that gets muddy for me.\n\nBut the voice, yeah… that’s what scared me enough to come in. It’s this man’s voice, and it sounds like it’s coming out of the garage vents sometimes, or even from under the hood when I’m working on a car. Like clear, not just a thought in my head—like somebody standing right next to me. He’ll say not to trust my husband, or not to touch certain cars.\n\nAnd then I start thinking, okay, was that during a stretch where I wasn’t using anything, or was it after I was around those people again, and I just… I can’t sort the calendar out right. But the voice has happened at work, around the cars and the vents.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Not every second, but it was still happening, coming through like the vents, and I still felt like my husband and my boss were tracking me somehow. Like if I missed doing laundry, was that one weekend or two? And then I’m thinking about the uniform shirts piling up, which isn’t really what you asked.\n\nSo… I can’t honestly say it was nearly every day for at least two weeks. I know I’ve felt down and hopeless at times, but I don’t know how long exactly.\n\nDoctor: During one of those low periods, did you feel sad or hopeless most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say “most of the day nearly every day.” I remember feeling low, yeah, like that heavy feeling where you’re just kind of dragging through things, but when you ask it that exact way I start trying to count hours, and then I get lost.\n\nLike, was I sad all day, or was I just sad in the morning before work and then numb while I was doing brakes or whatever? Sometimes at the shop you’re busy enough that you don’t really know what you feel until later, when it’s quiet. And then I’ll sit in the car too long before going inside, but I don’t know if that means most of the day.\n\nSo… I’m not saying no. I just don’t really know how to answer it cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you felt sad or hopeless nearly every day for two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking the same thing, and I’m not trying to dodge it. I mean, I’ve had times where food just sounded like too much effort, but I don’t know if that was no appetite or just… you know, not wanting to deal with making something.\n\nSometimes at work I’ll forget to eat until later because we’re busy, and then I’m like, okay, is that part of the low mood or is that just the shop being chaotic? A whole month feels like a big chunk to remember cleanly, and I don’t really have it marked out like that.\n\nLike, I can think of periods where I wasn’t around certain people as much, or I was just going to work and coming home, but then I start second-guessing whether that was actually a full month or just a couple weeks that felt longer. I don’t know.\n\nDoctor: During the past 10 months, did you ever hear the voice at a time when you had been off methamphetamine for several weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can line it up with being off meth for several weeks.
